--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Mission Analysis:**
- Before switch time: penalized for moving → robots should STOP
- After switch time: penalized for moving → wait, robots should MOVE
- Switch is signaled by a GREEN wall lighting up (color 2 in ARGoS? Let me think... colors: 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)
- The green wall is the signal to start moving

**Key insight:** The best FSM so far is pure Exploration (B=0) with score 16583. This seems counterintuitive - why would moving be good? 

Wait, let me re-read: "score increases by 1 for every robot that is stationary" BEFORE switch, and "score increases by 1 for every robot that is moving" AFTER switch.

So the optimal strategy is: **STOP before switch, MOVE after switch**. The green wall signals the switch.

The current best (pure Exploration) scores ~16583. A pure Stop (B=1) would score ~0 before switch but ~28*600 = 16800 after switch → roughly similar! The scoring is symmetric around the switch.

The ideal FSM: **State 0 = Stop, transition to State 1 = Exploration when green is perceived (C=7, l=2)**

Let me also think about what color "green" is: the problem says "lights up GREEN" - in ARGoS color encoding for condition C=7, we need the color index. Green should be color 2.

**Strategy:**
1. Keep champion (pure Exploration)
2. Try the ideal 2-state: Stop → (see green) → Explore
3. Try variants with different parameters
4. Try 3-state machines with more robustness

For C=7 (perceives green color), l=2 (green).

The switch happens between 400-600 steps. Before that, Stop is optimal. After green appears, Exploration is optimal.

Let me design the population:

1. **Champion** (exact copy): pure Exploration
2. **Ideal 2-state**: Stop → see green → Explore
3. **Variants** of the ideal 2-state with different probabilities
4. **3-state** machines for robustness
5. **Pure Stop** (might be good if switch is late)
6. Some exploitation of other top performers

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key insights from Round 2:**
- Champion is pure Stop (B=1) with score 13777.65 - this is surprisingly good
- The 2-state Stop→(see green)→Explore is second best at 13869.86
- Pure Exploration dropped to score 16470 (worse)
- The green-detection approach works but pure Stop is still better

**Why pure Stop wins:** Before switch (~500 steps average), all robots stationary = 0 penalty. After switch (~700 steps), all robots stationary = 28*700 = 19600 penalty. But pure Stop scores ~13778, which is less than 19600... This means the switch timing randomness and the fact that robots need to perceive green (which requires being near the wall) makes the green detection unreliable.

Wait - actually pure Stop score of 13777 makes sense: average switch at step 500, so after switch ~700 steps × 28 robots = 19600 score from moving penalty, but robots are stopped = 0 before. So 13777 < 19600 means... hmm, or maybe the scoring is: score = steps_stopped_before + steps_moving_after. Pure Stop: score ≈ 0 + 28×700 = 19600? But score is 13777...

Actually: score = (steps where stationary before switch) + (steps where moving after switch). Pure Stop: before switch no penalty (stopped=good), after switch stopped robots = penalty every step × 28. Average: 28 × (1200-500) = 19600. But score is 13777 < 19600. So some robots must be moving sometimes even when stopped? Or I'm misreading.

Re-reading: "score increases by 1 for every robot that is stationary" before switch. So Stop is BAD before switch! And moving is bad after switch. So optimal: MOVE before, STOP after.

That means: Pure Stop scores 28×500 (average) = 14000 before switch + 0 after = ~14000. That matches 13777!

Pure Explore: 0 before + 28×700 = 19600 after. But scored 16470... maybe explore doesn't move all robots always.

So the GREEN signal means: switch to STOP when you see green!

**Revised strategy:** State 0 = Explore (before switch), State 1 = Stop (after seeing green). This should score near 0!

The champion (pure Stop) scores ~13777 because it's wrong half the time. The ideal is Explore → (see green) → Stop.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Analysis of champion (score 11806):**
- State 0: Exploration (rwm=57), transitions to State 1 (Stop) on seeing green (C=7, p=0.95) OR on many neighbors (C=3, threshold=5, w=10)
- State 1: Stop, transitions back to State 0 on random probability (C=5, p=0.001)
- State 2: GoToColor green (B=8), transitions to State 1 on seeing green (C=7, p=0.9)

The 3rd state (GoToColor green) seems to help robots find the green wall faster, then stop. This is clever: robots move toward green when they see it, confirm they see it, then stop.

**Key observations:**
- The champion uses neighbor count as a secondary trigger to stop (crowding signal)
- The GoToColor state helps move toward the green signal
- Very small probability (0.001) to leave Stop state prevents accidental restarts
- Score of 11806 vs pure Stop at 14174 = significant improvement

**Exploitation ideas:**
1. Tweak the champion's parameters (p values, threshold, rwm)
2. Try higher/lower green detection probability
3. Try different neighbor thresholds
4. Add more transitions to the Stop state (make it stickier)
5. Try 4-state machines with more sophisticated behavior

**Key design principle:** Robots should explore/move before switch, detect green, go toward it, then stop permanently.

The GoToColor state (state 2 in champion) has LED color 0 (off) - the cle2=2 error I made last round. Valid LED colors are 0,4,5,6.

Let me design 14 machines:

1. Champion (exact copy)
2-5. Exploitation: tweak champion parameters
6-9. More exploitation variants
10-14. Exploration with different topologies

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Champion analysis (score 10229):**
- State 0: Exploration (rwm=57), transitions to State 1 (Stop) on seeing green (p=0.95) OR many neighbors (threshold=5, w=10)
- State 1: Stop, transitions to State 0 on random (p=0.001) OR back to State 1 on seeing green (p=0.05) - this second transition is self-referential... wait, n1x1 destination=1, but state 1 only has "other" states [0,2], so D=1 means state 2!
- So State 1: Stop → State 0 (random, p=0.001) OR → State 2 (see green, p=0.05)
- State 2: GoToColor green, transitions to State 1 (Stop) on seeing green (p=0.9)

**Key insight:** When stopped robots see green, they go to GoToColor state first (move toward green), then stop again. This helps all robots find and confirm the green signal. The neighbor threshold also helps: if many robots bunch up, they stop (robots aggregate near green wall, detecting high neighbor count).

**Why score improved from 11806 to 10229:** The champion added a second transition from Stop state - seeing green from Stop triggers GoToColor (to better orient), then returns to Stop. This is clever.

**Exploitation ideas:**
1. Tune p values (0.05 for green detection from Stop, 0.95 for green from Explore)
2. Tune neighbor threshold (5) and steepness (10)
3. Try different rwm values
4. Try 4-state machines with more sophisticated routing
5. Try higher probability for green detection from Stop

**Key concern:** The neighbor threshold transition in State 0 might cause false positives (stopping before switch when robots cluster). But it seems to help overall.

Let me focus exploitation on:
- Varying the green detection probability from Stop (currently 0.05) - try 0.1, 0.2, 0.02
- Varying neighbor threshold from Explore (currently 5, w=10) - try 3,4,6,7
- Varying rwm (currently 57) - try 50, 65
- Adding transitions to GoToColor state

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Champion analysis (score 9002):**
- State 0: Explore (rwm=57), → State 1 (Stop) on green (p=0.95) OR → State 1 on neighbors≥3 (w=10)
- State 1: Stop, → State 0 on random (p=0.001) OR → State 1 (self via D=1 meaning other state index) wait...

Let me re-read: with 3 states, leaving state 1, the "other" states are [0, 2]. So D=0→state0, D=1→state2.
- State 1: Stop, n1x0: D=0→State0 (random p=0.001), n1x1: D=1→State2 (see green p=0.05)
- State 2: GoToColor green, → State 1 (D=1, which from state2 means the other states [0,1], so D=1→State1) on seeing green (p=0.9)

**The pattern:** Explore → see green or crowd → Stop. From Stop, rarely go back to explore (p=0.001) or go to GoToColor when seeing green. GoToColor → back to Stop when confirmed green.

**Neighbor threshold 3 (not 5) is better** - lower threshold means robots stop sooner when they cluster, which happens near the green wall.

**Key parameters to tune:**
- Neighbor threshold: currently 3, try 2, 4
- Steepness w: currently 10, try 8, 12, 15
- Green detection from explore: p=0.95, try 0.9, 1.0
- Green detection from GoToColor: p=0.9, try 0.95, 1.0
- rwm: 57, try 45, 65, 80
- The "go back to explore" probability: 0.001, try 0.0005, 0.002

**Important note about n1x1:** In the champion, `--n1x1 1` with destination D=1 from state 1 means state 2 (the other states from state 1 are [0,2], D=0=state0, D=1=state2). So Stop→GoToColor when seeing green. This is correct.

**Avoid:** 4-state machines (performed poorly), extra transitions in GoToColor state (worst performer).

## Reasoning

**Situation assessment:** The champion (9002.76) has held for 3 rounds. We're in round 8 of 10 - only 2 rounds left after this one. Need to push harder for improvement.

**Key observations:**
- Adding a second transition to GoToColor state (n2x1: random restart p=0.001) gave score 9116 - close to champion but not better
- The topology is very stable: the 3-state Explore→Stop→GoToColor loop
- Champion uses: rwm=57, green-detect p=0.95, neighbor threshold=3, w=10, random-from-stop p=0.001, green-from-stop p=0.05, green-from-GoToColor p=0.9

**What hasn't been tried:**
- Neighbor threshold=1 or 2 from GoToColor state (more aggressive stopping after reaching green)
- Different neighbor thresholds in GoToColor state as 2nd transition
- Combining the GoToColor 2nd transition (random restart) with slightly different main params
- Try threshold=3, w=10 from GoToColor as additional stop trigger
- Try starting in GoToColor state (if green visible immediately, go to it)
- Fine-tune: p=0.06 for green-from-stop, w=11,12,13 for neighbor threshold

**New idea:** What if state 2 GoToColor has neighbor-count transition as well? If robots see many neighbors near green wall, they stop more reliably.

**Also:** Let me try having state 0 with 3 transitions: green→Stop, neighbors→Stop, AND green→GoToColor all separate (but that's duplicate condition C=7, not sure if allowed).

Actually looking at the 3-transition variant from round 6 (9316): `--n0 3 ... --n0x2 1 --c0x2 7 --p0x2 0.8` - this had a 3rd green detection going to Stop (same destination as first). That's different from having GoToColor as destination.

Let me try the GoToColor 2nd transition variant more carefully, and also combine with parameter tweaks.

## Reasoning

**Situation:** Round 9 of 10. Champion stuck at 9002.76 for 4 rounds. Two rounds left (this one and round 10). Need to break through.

**Analysis of what works:**
- The 3-state topology (Explore→Stop→GoToColor) is solid
- Best variants cluster around 9183-9265
- The GoToColor 2nd transition (random restart p=0.001) helps slightly
- Neighbor threshold=3, w=10-11 is optimal
- rwm=57 is best
- Green detection from Explore: p=0.95 is best
- Green detection from Stop: p=0.05-0.07 (0.07 gave 9183, best this round)
- Neighbor threshold in GoToColor transition to Stop is BAD (worst performers)

**Critical insight:** The champion uses GoToColor→Stop on green (p=0.9). The second best this round (9183) has GoToColor with TWO transitions: green→Stop (p=0.9) AND random restart (p=0.001). But the champion (9002) only has ONE transition in GoToColor. The champion was from round 5 and has been the best consistently - the GoToColor 2nd transition variants are consistently ~200 worse.

**New ideas to try:**
1. Try p=0.08, 0.09, 0.1 for green-from-Stop (n1x1)
2. Try w=9 for neighbor threshold
3. Try GoToColor→Stop on neighbor count (C=3) - not tried properly yet with correct destination
4. Try 4-state machine: Explore → GoToColor (on seeing green) → Stop (confirmed green) with extra state
5. Try having neighbor threshold go to GoToColor instead of Stop from Explore state

**Important:** I notice the GoToColor+neighbor-count 2nd transition was a WORST performer. But random restart from GoToColor is decent. Let me focus on fine-tuning p1x1 (green-from-stop) toward 0.08-0.1.

Also trying: What if from Explore, neighbor threshold sends to GoToColor (state 2) instead of Stop (state 1)? This way crowded robots go toward green rather than stopping blindly.

## Reasoning

**Final round (10 of 10).** Champion stuck at 9002.76. Need to maximize chances of improvement.

**What we know:**
- Core topology: Explore(rwm=57) → [green p=0.95 OR neighbors≥3 w=10] → Stop → [random p=0.001 OR green p=0.05] → [Stop or GoToColor] → [green p=0.9] → Stop
- p1x1 (green-from-Stop to GoToColor): p=0.05 is champion; p=0.1 gave 9090, p=0.06 gave 9128 - all worse than champion but close
- 4-state machines perform poorly
- GoToColor with extra transitions performs poorly
- Neighbor threshold=3 w=10 is optimal

**Final push strategies:**
1. Keep champion exact
2. Try p1x1 values between 0.04 and 0.05 (e.g. 0.04, 0.045)
3. Try p2x0 (green-from-GoToColor) = 0.85, 0.95
4. Try neighbor threshold=3 with w=8, w=9 (not tried with current best params)
5. Try the 3-transition state0 variant more carefully
6. Try rwm=53, 55 (near 57)
7. Try combining p1x1=0.1 (best this round) with slightly different other params
8. Try GoToColor→Explore (D=0) instead of GoToColor→Stop (D=1) on green - different routing
9. Try p0x0=0.93 (slightly below 0.95)

**Key observation:** n0x1 destination D=1 means state 1 (Stop) from state 0. What if the neighbor threshold sends to GoToColor (D=1 from state 0 = state 1, but we want state 2 = D=1 from state 0 means... wait, from state 0, other states are [1,2], so D=0=state1, D=1=state2). 

So in the champion: `--n0x1 1 --c0x1 3` means D=1 = state 2 (GoToColor)! Not state 1 (Stop)! Wait no - the champion has `--n0x0 1` and `--n0x1 1` - both point to D=1? Let me re-read: `--n0x0 1 --c0x0 7` (green→D=1=state1=Stop? or state2?) and `--n0x1 1 --c0x1 3` (neighbors→D=1=same).

From state 0, other states are [1,2]: D=0→state1, D=1→state2. So both transitions go to state 2 (GoToColor)! That means the neighbor threshold also triggers GoToColor, not Stop directly. The champion never goes directly from Explore to Stop!

This is the key insight. Both triggers from Explore go to GoToColor. Then GoToColor→Stop. This is elegant.

So: Explore → (green OR crowd) → GoToColor → (green confirmed) → Stop → (random OR green) → GoToColor loop.

With this understanding, let me fine-tune around the champion more carefully.

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

Each run is divided into two phases by a switch time drawn uniformly at random between 400 and 600 simulation steps. The robots are not told when it occurs.

Before the switch time, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is stationary. From the switch time onwards, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is moving. A robot counts as moving if it travelled more than 0.0005 m since the previous step.

At the switch time, one wall block of the arena, chosen at random, lights up GREEN. The floor of the arena is uniformly GRAY.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 28 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.58 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
